[04:23:20] Each seat is based on five gallons a day per seat, not necessarily per person, right? So it's a per seat use. [04:23:42] Let's just assume conservative purposes, 50% of them go to the bathroom, right? I think we can all agree that not everyone goes to the bathroom when they're at the church, right? [04:24:05] So of 100 people are using the restroom, they're using maybe 200 gallons for that service. Next service comes in, same thing, another 200 gallons. So now we're up to 400 gallons. [04:24:26] So we haven't even reached that average daily on two services on Sunday, and Sunday is our biggest day of flow. We've designed the system. [06:09:29] for the proposed site. According to the staff report for the proposed building, the ORPA non-residential floor area ratio target is between 0 and 0.02. We could not find any references to what the calculated ratio is for the proposed building, but we can make an estimate as follows. To estimate the floor area ratio, we take the gross floor area of the building of 18.4 thousand square feet divided by the total lot size of four and a half acres, that is equivalent to 186,000 square feet,

[06:10:07] which results in an estimated floor area ratio value of 0.1. This is five times the maximum ORPA-targeted floor area ratio of 0.02 we mentioned above. The ratio estimate of 0.1 that the proposed site will have assumes a 100% buildable land space. If the actual buildable land space were less, say 75%, the rough average in Virginia, then the floor area ratio value would

[06:10:41] be larger by 6.6 times the ORPA target. At 50% of buildable land space, typical of non-flat parcels such as the proposed site, then the floor area ratio would be even higher at 10 times the ORPA target. Looking at it in a different way, for a building to meet the ORPA target in the proposed lot, assuming the 100% buildable land space, the maximum size of the building would have to be smaller than 3,700 square foot. [06:11:18] square feet. And finally, at 50 percent buildable land, which is more reflective of the proposed site, it would have to be smaller than 1,850 square feet. [08:49:04] impacts of any project, the two key issues to keep an eye on are safety and mobility. But before I get to that, I would like to mention that I'm not a stranger to Davis Ford Road. [08:49:34] Yes, Davis Ford Road has some safety concerns, just like any other road facility. However, the death trap tag, which has been amplified by the opposition, is not supported by data. From available VDOT crash data, the section of Davis Ford Road within the immediate vicinity of this project has experienced two fatal or severe crashes within the past five years.

[08:49:56] The remaining five crashes within the same time period are minor in nature and mostly involve vehicle runoffs or rear-end crashes without any driver or pedestrian injury. Per Federal Highway Safety Guidance, these numbers do not support a high crash location. The current safety situation will not be exacerbated by this project because of the fewer number of vehicles that will be added to the traffic mix.

[08:50:18] If we were to double the annual average daily traffic on Davis Ford Road, then I would agree that it will impact safety. But we are not doing that. Per our projected additional traffic of 114 vehicles on Sundays, vehicle headways will be around 32 seconds, meaning one vehicle passes every 32 seconds.

[08:50:37] The only way additional vehicles will significantly affect safety is if it results in vehicle headways less than two seconds. Clearly, this project will not adversely affect safety. In addition, the presence of off-duty police officers to control traffic on Sundays will further lessen the crash risk.

[08:50:57] For mobility, the key question to ask is, will this project cause extreme traffic congestion on the roadway? Currently, the speed limit is 45 miles per hour and vehicles operate in free-flow conditions, especially on Sundays. Considering the relatively fewer number of additional vehicles that will be introduced in the traffic mix, the current traffic flow conditions should not be negatively impacted.

[08:51:16] In addition, the provision of stacking lanes and tapers will ensure that turning traffic do not cause congestion. In summary, the Transformation Temple project will not have any adverse impacts on the Davis-Ford Road. Therefore, I pray that you all vote yes for this project.
